Couples can be Tractable: New Algorithms and Hardness Results for the Hospitals/Residents Problem with Couples
Authors: Gergely Csáji, David Manlove, Iain McBride, James Trimble
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We study the Hospitals/Residents problem with Couples (hrc), where a solution is a stable matching or a report that none exists. We present a novel polynomial-time algorithm that can find a near-feasible stable matching, where hospitals’ capacities are adjusted by at most 1, in an hrc instance where the couples’ preferences are sub-responsive (i.e., if one member switches to a better hospital, then the couple also improves) and sub-complete (i.e., each pair of hospitals that are individually acceptable to both members are jointly acceptable for the couple) by reducing it to an instance of the Stable Fixtures problem. We further present a polynomial-time algorithm for hrc in a sub-responsive, sub-complete instance that is a Dual Market, or where all couples are one of several possible types. We show that our algorithm also implies the polynomial-time solvability of a stable b-matching problem, where the underlying graph is a multigraph with loops. We complement our algorithms with several hardness results. We show that hrc with sub-responsive and sub-complete couples is NP-hard, even with other strong restrictions. We further show that hrc with a Dual Market is NP-hard under several simultaneous restrictions. Finally, we show that the problem of finding a matching with the minimum number of blocking pairs in hrc is not approximable within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m^{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}m1-ε, for any ɛ >0ε>0, where m is the total length of the hospitals’ preference lists, unless P=NP, even if each couple applies to only one pair of hospitals. Our polynomial-time solvability results greatly expand the class of known tractable instances of hrc and provide a useful tool for designing better and more efficient mechanisms in the future.

Introduction

Background

The Stable Marriage problem, and its many-to-one extension, the Hospitals/Residents problem (hr), are well-studied and central problems in Computational Social Choice, Computer Science, Game Theory and Economics, with numerous applications including in entry-level labour markets, school choice and higher education allocation [23]. In the medical sphere, centralised matching schemes that assign aspiring junior doctors to hospitals operate in many countries. One of the largest and best known examples is the National Resident Matching Program (NRMP) in the US [37], which had just under 45,000 applicants for just over 41,500 positions in the Main Residency Match in 2024 [38]. There are analogous matching schemes for junior doctor allocation in Canada [39], France [40] and Japan [41].

The hr problem model represents a bipartite matching market with two-sided preferences, involving the preferences of doctors over acceptable hospitals, and those of hospitals over their applicants. Each hospital has a capacity, indicating the maximum number of doctors that it can admit. Roth [31] argued that a key property to be satisfied by a matching M in an instance I of hr is stability, which ensures that there is no blocking pair, comprising a doctor and a hospital, both of whom have an incentive to deviate from their assignments in M and become matched to one another, undermining the integrity of M. It is known that every instance of hr admits a stable matching, which may be found in time linear in the size of I [13].

hr is also known to have very nice mathematical structure. In particular, the famous Rural Hospitals Theorem states that, for an arbitrary hr instance I, any doctor that is assigned in one stable matching in I is assigned in all stable matchings in I, moreover any hospital that is undersubscribed in some stable matching in I is assigned exactly the same set of doctors in every stable matching in I [14, 31, 32].

In many of the above applications, there may be couples amongst the applying doctors, who wish to be allocated to hospitals that are geographically close to each other, for example. Indeed, the NRMP matching algorithm was redesigned in 1983 specifically to allow couples to provide preferences over pairs of hospitals, with each pair representing a simultaneous assignment that is suitable for both members of the couple. We thus obtain a generalisation of hr called the Hospitals/Residents problem with Couples (hrc). By modifying the definition of stability, taking into account how a couple could improve relative to a matching, Roth [31] showed that the addition of couples destroys the crucial property that a stable matching must always exist. In hrc, the problem therefore is to find a stable matching or report that none exists. Even when a stable matching does exist, such matchings may have different sizes [1], thus showing that the Rural Hospitals Theorem does not hold in general for hrc. Worse still, and again in contrast to the case for hr, Ronn [30] showed that hrc is NP-hard.

Since then, further NP-hardness results for hrc have been established, even in very restrictive cases [6, 27, 30], suggesting that hrc is a very challenging computational problem in theory. Even so, the algorithm employed by the NRMP has succeeded in producing a stable matching every year (allowing for some small capacity adjustments for hospitals that may sometimes be necessary) since couples were first included in 1983. The NRMP employs a heuristic that is based on the Roth-Peranson algorithm described in [33].

The success of the NRMP (and that of similar schemes) is demonstrated by its longevity, celebrating its 70th anniversary in 2022, and its high level of participation, growing year on year to the current numbers reported above. Indeed, the contribution of Roth and Shapley to the theory of stable matching and the practice of centralised matching programmes such as the NRMP led to their award of the Nobel Prize in Economic Sciences in 2012 [42].

Nevertheless, up until now there have been very few special cases of hrc that have been shown to admit polynomial-time algorithms. Our work in this paper extends the techniques that can be used in hrc instances by providing novel polynomial-time algorithms for many practically important variants of the problem in a surprisingly wide range of settings, and thus contributes new ways to efficiently handle specific instances in several applications.

Related Work

Roth [31] considered stability in the hrc context although did not define the concept explicitly. Whilst Gusfield and Irving [15] defined stability formally in hrc, their definition neglected to deal with the case that both members of a couple may wish to be assigned to the same hospital simultaneously. There are discussions of this point in [5] and [23, Section 5.3.2]. McDermid and Manlove [25] provided a stability definition for hrc that does deal with this possibility. Other such definitions have been proposed in the literature [4, 22], and a detailed comparative study of the effects of different stability definitions for hrc was carried out by Delorme et al. [10].

In this paper, we will adopt the stability definition of McDermid and Manlove [25]. With respect to this definition, several algorithmic results for hrc hold. Firstly, Ronn’s NP-hardness result for hrc holds even in the case that each hospital has capacity 1 and there are no single doctors [30] (note that all hrc stability definitions are equivalent in the unit hospital capacity case, or in the case that no couple wish to be assigned to the same hospital). Ng and Hirschberg [27] (reported in [28]) showed that NP-hardness also holds additionally even when each couple finds acceptable every distinct pair of hospitals.

Ng and Hirschberg [27] also proved NP-hardness for a “dual market” restriction of hrc, which we refer to as hrc-dual market, where the two sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_1$$\end{document}H1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_2$$\end{document}H2, comprising the hospitals appearing in the first (respectively second) positions of the acceptable pairs of the first (respectively second) members of each couple are disjoint. This reflects the “two body” problem that often exists for couples, where, for example, one member of a couple may be applying for a job at a university, whilst the other couple member may be applying for a job in industry. Another motivating application arises in a student allocation scheme, where students apply to both a university degree programme and to an internship at a company. This can also be viewed as a specific hrc-dual market instance, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_1$$\end{document}H1 comprises the university degree programmes, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_2$$\end{document}H2 comprises the companies, and each student corresponds to a couple with one member applying to universities and the other to internships. Ng and Hirschberg’s NP-hardness result for hrc-dual market holds even if each hospital has capacity 1 and there are no single doctors.

McDermid and Manlove [25] showed that hrc is NP-hard even if each single doctor ranks at most three hospitals, each couple ranks at most two pairs of hospitals, each hospital ranks at most four doctors, and each hospital has a capacity of 1 or 2. Later, Biró et al. [6] strengthened this result by showing that NP-hardness holds for hrc even when there are no single doctors, each couple ranks at most two pairs of hospitals, each hospital ranks at most two doctors, and all hospitals have capacity 1. On the other hand, if each hospital finds at most one doctor acceptable, and there is no restriction on the lengths of the preference lists of single doctors and couples, Manlove et al. [24] showed that this special case of hrc admits a unique stable matching, which may be found in polynomial time. They also showed that hrc is solvable in polynomial time when each single doctor and hospital has a preference list of length at most two, and each couple finds only one hospital pair acceptable. Another simple tractable case of hrc is given by Klaus and Klijn [20] (see also [21]), but also in a very restricted framework. They required that each couple’s preference list must be weakly responsive (informally, a couple’s preferences are weakly responsive if there are underlying preferences over individual hospitals for each member of the couple, such that if one member of the couple goes to a better hospital then the couple also improves), each couple must find acceptable all possible outcomes where at least one member is matched, and each hospital has capacity 1.

Given the prevalence of NP-hardness results for hrc, and the scarcity of polynomial-time algorithms, heuristics have been applied to the problem (see [23, Section 5.3,3] for a survey) as well as approaches based on parameterised complexity and local search [4, 26], integer programming [6, 10], constraint programming [24] and SAT solving [11].

Nguyen and Vohra [29] studied so-called near-feasible stable matchings in hrc. They showed that if one can modify the capacities of the hospitals by at most 2, then a stable matching with respect to the new capacities always exists (the new total hospital capacity is at least as large as before and increases by at most 4). They also provided an algorithm to find such a near-feasible solution, however their algorithm is not guaranteed to run in polynomial time, as in the first step it computes a stable fractional matching using Scarf’s algorithm [35], and the computation of stable fractional matchings is known to be PPAD-hard [8]. Biró et al. [3] also used Scarf’s algorithm to find stable matchings in hrc instances.

Another direction, to cope with the possible non-existence of a stable matching, is to consider matchings that are “as stable as possible”, i.e., admit the minimum number of blocking pairs. We refer to this problem as min bp hrc. Biró et al. [6] showed that this problem is NP-hard and not approximable within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_D^{1-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}nD1-ε, for any ɛ >0ε>0, unless P=NP, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_D$$\end{document}nD is the number of doctors in a given instance. Manlove et al. [24] presented integer and constraint programming formulations for min bp hrc.

Our Contributions

In this paper, we provide new polynomial-time algorithms for hrc in the case that the couples’ preference lists are sub-complete and sub-responsive. Informally, a couple’s preferences are sub-complete if there are underlying preferences over individual hospitals for each couple member, such that if both members go to a hospital that is acceptable for them individually, then the pair of hospitals is also acceptable for the couple, and if one member is willing to be unmatched, then any assignment of the other member to an acceptable hospital is acceptable for the couple. Sub-responsiveness requires that, for two acceptable pairs of positions for a couple, such that one member is in the same hospital in both, the couple prefers the pair in which the other member is in a better hospital according to his/her underlying preference list. The concept of sub-responsiveness, even together with sub-completeness, is closely related to, but a lot less restrictive than weak responsiveness as described above; with the latter concept having been extensively studied by economists [20, 34]. The main difference is that weakly responsive preferences require that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h\succ _{c_i} \emptyset $$\end{document}h≻ci∅ for a member \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i$$\end{document}ci of a couple C, then the pair (h,\emptyset )(h,∅) is acceptable for C. This forbids the option for a couple to only accept assignments where both members receive positions. In fact, this is one of the main features that makes hrc computationally hard. With sub-responsive and sub-complete preferences, couples do still have this option.

The contributions of this paper are as follows.A polynomial-time algorithm to find a near feasible stable matching in hrc , given sub-complete and sub-responsive preferences.Our main result is a novel and surprising polynomial-time algorithm to find a near-feasible stable matching in an hrc instance if the couples’ preferences are sub-responsive and sub-complete. In this paper, our notion of near-feasibility is based on modifying the capacities of each hospital by at most 1. This is inspired by the work of Nguyen and Vohra’s [29]. On the one hand, our algorithm only works for a special case of hrc, and the total change of capacities are not bounded, as opposed to [29]. On the other hand, in our algorithm, (i) individual capacities are varied by at most 1 rather than 2, and (ii) our algorithm runs in polynomial time. This result is established via a reduction to the Stable Fixtures problem (sf), a non-bipartite many-to-many generalisation of hr [19]. Our algorithm has other nice properties: for example, it can indeed be very beneficial for a couple to apply together, as it can happen that by applying alone, only one of them would have been accepted, but by applying together, both of them are. We later illustrate this property in Example 3.6.A polynomial-time algorithm for hrc , assuming couples belong to fixed types and an extension of the Rural-Hospitals Theorem.We provide another polynomial-time algorithm for hrc in the presence of sub-responsive and sub-complete preferences that can find a stable matching if all couples are one of several possible types, which we refer to as type-a, type-b and type-c. These types are defined formally in Sect. 2, but may be informally described as follows. A sub-responsive and sub-complete couple is:type-a if the two members of the couple apply to distinct hospitals;type-b if the couple are connected (i.e., either they must both be matched or they must both be unmatched), they have exactly one hospital h in common that they both find acceptable, and the worse member of the couple for h applies only to h;type-c if the couple are connected, they have exactly one hospital h in common that they both find acceptable, and h is the worst hospital for both members of the couple. Detailed motivation for these couple types is given in Sect. 1.4, but we briefly mention here that type-a corresponds to the very practical and natural restriction of hrc-dual market in the case of sub-responsive and sub-complete preference lists, and gives a contrast to the NP-hardness result of Ng and Hirschberg for general hrc-dual market instances as mentioned earlier. Using our approach, we argue that our algorithm can potentially be extended to other types of couples, depending on the specific application. On the structural side, we prove that a version of the Rural Hospitals Theorem remains true even in our hrc setting with sub-responsive and sub-complete preferences, and couples belonging to these types. These are the first non-trivial classes of hrc instances that we are aware of where these structural properties hold.A polynomial-time algorithm for Stable Multigraph b-Matching. We also show that our algorithm implies the polynomial-time solvability of a stable b-matching problem, where the underlying graph is a multigraph with loops, which we call Stable Multigraph b-Matching. This is a generalisation of the Stable Multiple Activities problem studied by Cechlárová and Fleiner [7] with loops also allowed, where a loop occupies two positions at its incident node.NP-hardness of hrc-dual with master lists and hrc with sub-complete and sub-responsive preferences. We complement our positive results with several hardness results. We show that hrc is NP-hard, even with unit hospital capacities, short preference lists and other strong (simultaneous) restrictions, including (i) sub-responsive and sub-complete couples, and (ii) dual markets and master preference lists [18]. Hence, even in these settings we may not hope to find an exact stable matching in polynomial time, so our algorithm to find a near-feasible stable solution becomes even more appealing.Strengthened inapproximability of min bp hrc . In this paper, our notion of near-feasibility is based on modifying the capacities of each hospital by at most 1. This is inspired by the work of Nguyen and Vohra’s [29]. On the one hand, our algorithm only works for a special case of hrc, and the total change of capacities are not bounded, as opposed to [29]. A polynomial-time algorithm to find a near feasible stable matching in hrc , given sub-complete and sub-responsive preferences.

Our main result is a novel and surprising polynomial-time algorithm to find a near-feasible stable matching in an hrc instance if the couples’ preferences are sub-responsive and sub-complete. In this paper, our notion of near-feasibility is based on modifying the capacities of each hospital by at most 1. This is inspired by the work of Nguyen and Vohra’s [29]. On the one hand, our algorithm only works for a special case of hrc, and the total change of capacities are not bounded, as opposed to [29]. On the other hand, in our algorithm, (i) individual capacities are varied by at most 1 rather than 2, and (ii) our algorithm runs in polynomial time. This result is established via a reduction to the Stable Fixtures problem (sf), a non-bipartite many-to-many generalisation of hr [19]. Our algorithm has other nice properties: for example, it can indeed be very beneficial for a couple to apply together, as it can happen that by applying alone, only one of them would have been accepted, but by applying together, both of them are. We later illustrate this property in Example 3.6.

A polynomial-time algorithm for hrc , assuming couples belong to fixed types and an extension of the Rural-Hospitals Theorem.We provide another polynomial-time algorithm for hrc in the presence of sub-responsive and sub-complete preferences that can find a stable matching if all couples are one of several possible types, which we refer to as type-a, type-b and type-c. These types are defined formally in Sect. 2, but may be informally described as follows. A sub-responsive and sub-complete couple is:type-a if the two members of the couple apply to distinct hospitals;type-b if the couple are connected (i.e., either they must both be matched or they must both be unmatched), they have exactly one hospital h in common that they both find acceptable, and the worse member of the couple for h applies only to h;type-c if the couple are connected, they have exactly one hospital h in common that they both find acceptable, and h is the worst hospital for both members of the couple. Detailed motivation for these couple types is given in Sect. 1.4, but we briefly mention here that type-a corresponds to the very practical and natural restriction of hrc-dual market in the case of sub-responsive and sub-complete preference lists, and gives a contrast to the NP-hardness result of Ng and Hirschberg for general hrc-dual market instances as mentioned earlier. Using our approach, we argue that our algorithm can potentially be extended to other types of couples, depending on the specific application. On the structural side, we prove that a version of the Rural Hospitals Theorem remains true even in our hrc setting with sub-responsive and sub-complete preferences, and couples belonging to these types. These are the first non-trivial classes of hrc instances that we are aware of where these structural properties hold.

A polynomial-time algorithm for Stable Multigraph b-Matching. We also show that our algorithm implies the polynomial-time solvability of a stable b-matching problem, where the underlying graph is a multigraph with loops, which we call Stable Multigraph b-Matching. This is a generalisation of the Stable Multiple Activities problem studied by Cechlárová and Fleiner [7] with loops also allowed, where a loop occupies two positions at its incident node.

A polynomial-time algorithm for Stable Multigraph b-Matching. We also show that our algorithm implies the polynomial-time solvability of a stable b-matching problem, where the underlying graph is a multigraph with loops, which we call Stable Multigraph b-Matching. This is a generalisation of the Stable Multiple Activities problem studied by Cechlárová and Fleiner [7] with loops also allowed, where a loop occupies two positions at its incident node.

NP-hardness of hrc-dual with master lists and hrc with sub-complete and sub-responsive preferences. We complement our positive results with several hardness results. We show that hrc is NP-hard, even with unit hospital capacities, short preference lists and other strong (simultaneous) restrictions, including (i) sub-responsive and sub-complete couples, and (ii) dual markets and master preference lists [18]. Hence, even in these settings we may not hope to find an exact stable matching in polynomial time, so our algorithm to find a near-feasible stable solution becomes even more appealing.

NP-hardness of hrc-dual with master lists and hrc with sub-complete and sub-responsive preferences. We complement our positive results with several hardness results. We show that hrc is NP-hard, even with unit hospital capacities, short preference lists and other strong (simultaneous) restrictions, including (i) sub-responsive and sub-complete couples, and (ii) dual markets and master preference lists [18]. Hence, even in these settings we may not hope to find an exact stable matching in polynomial time, so our algorithm to find a near-feasible stable solution becomes even more appealing.

A summary of results in this paper. hrc-near-feasible denotes the problem of finding a near-feasible stable matching in a hrc instance, which means that the capacities of the hospitals are allowed to be changed by at most the value indicated

The results in this paper are summarised in Table 1. The polynomial-time algorithms in this paper substantially push forward our knowledge of tractable special cases of hrc. Moreover they give additional evidence as to why successful matching schemes such as the NRMP have continued to find stable matchings even in the presence of couples. Our algorithm for the case that the couples’ preferences are sub-responsive and sub-complete, and the couples are one of several possible types, also helps to identify the frontier between polynomial-time solvable and NP-hard cases, as our hardness results show that if we have weaker restrictions on the couples’ preferences, then it becomes NP-hard to find a stable matching.

Motivation for Couple Types

In this subsection we provide some detailed motivation for the couple types described informally above, and defined formally below in Definitions 2.2 and 2.3. Firstly, in the Dual Market example given in Sect. 1, where each “couple” is a student who applies to both a university and a company, the underlying mathematical problem is hrc-dual market. It is natural to assume that preferences are sub-responsive and sub-complete in this case, and all couples are thus of type-a as observed above.

Types-b and -c may seem a little more artificial. On the one hand, they will demonstrate the flexibility of our technique such that it can handle couples even if they apply to the same hospital. On the other hand, there may be applications related to hrc where these types of (sub-responsive and sub-complete) structures arise, especially in scenarios where it is better for a couple (or an agent) to obtain a joint allocation where the two assigned positions are more diverse. Imagine for example that two members of a couple apply to part-time positions, which also have associated shifts (indicating which days they have to attend). Then, a couple may prefer their shifts to be on different days, to minimise the amount of daycare needed for their children. For example, if one of them applies to shifts on days 1, 2 and 3, and the other on days 3, 4 and 5, then it is natural to assume that they express a sub-responsive and sub-complete preference list, such that (3,3) is ranked last, hence they give a type-c couple.

Another motivation may come from school choice, with pairs of siblings applying at the same time. There, since usually most applications are to nearby schools anyway, and an older child can travel independently, sub-completeness and sub-responsiveness are more natural for many participants. Furthermore, a joint sub-responsive and sub-complete list still allows features like prioritising one of the children within the ranking, say the younger child being assigned to a school close to his/her parents’ workplaces, or the elder child being assigned to a top school to improve his/her chances of being admitted to a good university later.

We also note the important special case of hrc in which each couple applies only to one pair of hospitals. This restriction is polynomial-time solvable, since each couple is of type-a or -b, as we show later in Corollary 3.10. On the other hand, hrc is NP-hard even if each couple finds acceptable only two hospital pairs [6]. This helps to push forward our understanding of the frontier between polynomial-time solvability and NP-hardness for restrictions of hrc. Indeed, couple structures that do not make the problem NP-hard may need to be very specific, which is also supported by Theorem 4.1, where we prove that hrc remains NP-hard even with sub-responsive and sub-complete couples, such that each member of a couple applies to only two hospitals.

As a more theoretical application, couples types-a and -b also arise in Stable Multigraph b-Matching (see Corollary 3.12.)

Finally, we note that even in cases where there may be different types of couples (beyond the type-a, -b and -c couples defined in Definitions 2.2 and 2.3), it may speed up heuristics and IP techniques to first compute (in polynomial time) a stable solution restricted to the single doctors and the couples of types-a, -b and -c.

Structure of the Paper

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows. Section 2 provides definitions of key notation, terminology and problem models. Our polynomial-time algorithms and structural results are then presented in Sect. 3, whilst our NP-hardness and inapproximability results appear in Sect. 4. Finally, Sect. 5 gives some conclusions and open problems.

An hrc instance can therefore be represented as a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=(D,C,H,\textbf{q},\succ )$$\end{document}I=(D,C,H,q,≻), where D is the set of single doctors, C is the set of couples, H is the set of hospitals, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{q}$$\end{document}q is the capacity vector for the hospitals, and ≻ ≻ represents the preference lists of single doctors, couples and hospitals.

We now define the concept of a blocking pair in hrc, adopting the definition from [25]. While the definition seems complicated, the underlying idea of it is just to consider the possible cases for single doctors, couples and hospitals to form a mutually beneficial deviation from the matching.

In hrc, the problem is to find a stable matching or report that none exists. An important special case of hrc arises when the set H of hospitals can be partitioned into two sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_1,H_2$$\end{document}H1,H2, such that for each couple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i\in C$$\end{document}Ci∈C, each acceptable pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(h_j,h_k)\in A(C_i)$$\end{document}(hj,hk)∈A(Ci) satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_j\in H_1$$\end{document}hj∈H1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_k\in H_2$$\end{document}hk∈H2, and for each single doctor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_i$$\end{document}di, either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A(d_i)\subseteq H_1$$\end{document}A(di)⊆H1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A(d_i)\subseteq H_2$$\end{document}A(di)⊆H2. We refer to this restriction of hrc as hrc-dual market (corresponding to a Dual Market).

Given that an instance I of hrc may not admit a stable matching, we define min bp hrc to be the problem of finding a matching in I with the minimum number of blocking pairs. Formally, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M denote the set of matchings in I. Given a matching \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\in \mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M∈M, let bp(I, M) denote the set of blocking pairs with respect to M in I. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$bp(I)=\min \{|bp(I,M)|: M\in \mathcal {M}\}$$\end{document}bp(I)=min{|bp(I,M)|:M∈M}. Define min bp hrc to be the problem of finding, given an hrc instance I, a matching \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\in \mathcal {M}$$\end{document}M∈M such that |bp(I,M)|=bp(I)|bp(I,M)|=bp(I).

Next we define some properties regarding the preference lists of couples.

Definition 2.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i=(c_{2i-1},c_{2i})$$\end{document}Ci=(c2i-1,c2i) be a couple with joint preference list \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\succ _{C_i}$$\end{document}≻Ci. That is, if both members go to an acceptable hospital for them, then the pair of hospitals is also acceptable for the couple, and if one member is willing to be unmatched, then any assignment of the other member to an acceptable hospital is acceptable for the couple.We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i$$\end{document}Ci is sub-responsive or sub-complete if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\succ _{C_i}$$\end{document}≻Ci is sub-responsive or sub-complete, respectively.

We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} That is, if both members go to an acceptable hospital for them, then the pair of hospitals is also acceptable for the couple, and if one member is willing to be unmatched, then any assignment of the other member to an acceptable hospital is acceptable for the couple.

We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\succ _{C_i}$$\end{document}≻Ci is sub-complete if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A(C_i)=(A'(c_{2i-1})\times A'(c_{2i}))\setminus \{(\emptyset ,\emptyset )\}$$\end{document}A(Ci)=(A′(c2i-1)×A′(c2i))\{(∅,∅)}, where, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \{0,1\}$$\end{document}j∈{0,1}, either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A'(c_{2i-j})=A(c_{2i-j})$$\end{document}A′(c2i-j)=A(c2i-j) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A'(c_{2i-j})=A(c_{2i-j})\cup \{\emptyset \}$$\end{document}A′(c2i-j)=A(c2i-j)∪{∅}. That is, if both members go to an acceptable hospital for them, then the pair of hospitals is also acceptable for the couple, and if one member is willing to be unmatched, then any assignment of the other member to an acceptable hospital is acceptable for the couple.

As we show in Theorem 4.1, hrc remains NP-hard even with sub-responsive and sub-complete preference lists.

However, in Theorem 3.7, we provide a novel reduction to sf that allows us to deal with many different kinds of couples via a polynomial-time algorithm. To this end, we describe some special types of couples.

Even though these notions appear rather strict, we give a simple example to show that, even for a single couple of type-b, a stable matching may not exist.

Example 2.4

Consider an hrc instance comprising a couple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(c_1,c_2)$$\end{document}(c1,c2), a single doctor d, and a hospital h with capacity 2 and preference list \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1\succ _h {d}\succ _h c_2$$\end{document}c1≻hd≻hc2. The only acceptable option for the couple is if both are admitted to h. This gives a type-b couple. Suppose M is a stable matching. Then, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({d},h)\notin M$$\end{document}(d,h)∉M, then (d, h) blocks M, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${d}\succ _h c_2$$\end{document}d≻hc2. However, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({d},h)\in M$$\end{document}(d,h)∈M, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(c_1,c_2)$$\end{document}(c1,c2) is not assigned, so the couple blocks M with h.

We will show that hrc is solvable in polynomial time for couples of type-a, -b or -c. Moreover for Dual Markets, every sub-responsive and sub-complete couple is necessarily of type-a. However, every couple being of type-a is more general than hrc-dual market, as it only means that for each couple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i$$\end{document}Ci, the acceptable hospitals for each member of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_i$$\end{document}Ci are two disjoint sets. To illustrate this with a simple example, imagine that there are three hospitals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1,h_2,h_3$$\end{document}h1,h2,h3 and three couples \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1,C_2,C_3$$\end{document}C1,C2,C3. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1 applies only to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(h_1,h_2)$$\end{document}(h1,h2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_2$$\end{document}C2 applies only to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(h_2,h_3)$$\end{document}(h2,h3), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_3$$\end{document}C3 applies only to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(h_3,h_1)$$\end{document}(h3,h1). It is easy to see that every couple is of type-a. However, the market is not dual, as for any possible partition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ h_1,h_2,h_3\}$$\end{document}{h1,h2,h3} into two parts, there will be an application where both hospitals are from the same part.

Our results will also imply a nice corollary about a stable matching problem in multigraphs, which we now define. The setting is a multigraph G=(V,E)G=(V,E) where loops are also allowed. Each node v∈ Vv∈V has a strict preference list \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\succ _v$$\end{document}≻v over the edges incident to v. We are also given a capacity b(v) for each v∈ Vv∈V. A b-matching is a set M⊆ EM⊆E such that |M(v)|≤ b(v)|M(v)|≤b(v) for each v∈ Vv∈V, where M(v) is the set of edges in M that are incident to v. In this multigraph b-matching model, stability is defined as follows.

Definition 2.5

Let M be a b-matching in a multigraph G (possibly with loops). We say that an edge e=(u,v)∉ Me=(u,v)∉M (possibly u=vu=v) blocks M if one of the following conditions holds:u≠ vu≠v, and for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \{u,v\}$$\end{document}x∈{u,v}, either |M(x)|<b(x)|M(x)|<b(x) or there exists an edge f∈ M(x)f∈M(x) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} and b(u)-|M(u)|≥ 2b(u)-|M(u)|≥2;u=vu=v, and b(u)-|M(u)|≥ 1b(u)-|M(u)|≥1, and there exists an edge f∈ M(u)f∈M(u) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\succ _u f$$\end{document}e≻uf;u=vu=v, and there is a distinct edge f=(u,u)∈ Mf=(u,u)∈M such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\succ _u f$$\end{document}e≻uf;u=vu=v, and there are two edges f∈ M(u)f∈M(u) and g∈ M(u)g∈M(u) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} is no edge blocking M.

u≠ vu≠v, and for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \{u,v\}$$\end{document}x∈{u,v}, either |M(x)|<b(x)|M(x)|<b(x) or there exists an edge f∈ M(x)f∈M(x) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\succ _x f$$\end{document}e≻xf;

u=vu=v, and b(u)-|M(u)|≥ 1b(u)-|M(u)|≥1, and there exists an edge f∈ M(u)f∈M(u) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\succ _u f$$\end{document}e≻uf;

u=vu=v, and there are two edges f∈ M(u)f∈M(u) and g∈ M(u)g∈M(u) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\succ _u f$$\end{document}e≻uf and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\succ _u g$$\end{document}e≻ug.

We now define a decision problem based on the existence of a stable b-matching in this multigraph setting.

The Stable Fixtures problem (sf) [19], also called the Stable b-Matching problem, is defined similarly, but the underlying graph G is a simple graph, so it is not allowed to have loops or parallel edges, and hence the notion of stability also simplifies. The generalisation of sf where parallel edges are allowed, but loops are not, has been studied by Cechlárová and Fleiner [7], who called this problem Stable Multiple Activities. We note that our new problem variant does not reduce straightforwardly to Stable Multiple Activities. The main difficulty lies in the fact that for a loop edge e=(u,u)e=(u,u) to block, it needs two worse edges or free positions at node u, so if u is filled with better edges than e except for one worse edge, still it is the case that e is not allowed to block.

A special case of sf arises when b(v)=1b(v)=1 for all v∈ Vv∈V – this is the classical Stable Roommates problem (sr) [13, 16]. Tan [36] gave a polynomial-time algorithm to find a stable half-integral matching (an assignment in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}$$\end{document}{0,12,1} to each edge, such that each edge with weight less than 1 in the matching is dominated, meaning that at least one endpoint is saturated with at least as good – possibly fractional – edges) in any sr instance. This algorithm was extended to the sf setting by Fleiner [12].

Polynomial-Time Algorithms

In this section we describe our main positive results. We start by describing our most practical polynomial-time algorithm that is able to find a near-feasible stable matching in any hrc instance I, where the participating couples have sub-responsive and sub-complete preference lists.

Theorem 3.1

Given an hrc instance where each couple’s preferences are sub-responsive and sub-complete, there is always a near-feasible capacity vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {q}}'$$\end{document}q′ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|{\textbf {q}}'-{\textbf {q}}|_{\infty }\le 1$$\end{document}|q′-q|∞≤1, such that there is a stable matching with respect to the modified capacities. Furthermore, these modified capacities and the stable matching can be found in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(m)$$\end{document}O(m) time, where m denotes the total length of the preference lists of the hospitals.

Proof

We prove our statement by providing a polynomial-time reduction of the problem to sf. sf is known to have an algorithm with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(|E|)$$\end{document}O(|E|) running time [19], where E is the set of edges in the underlying graph in the sf instance. Then, we proceed by finding a fractional stable solution and round it off in a specific way to obtain a near-feasible stable matching.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=(D,C,H,\textbf{q},\succ )$$\end{document}I=(D,C,H,q,≻) be an instance of hrc where the couples’ preferences are sub-responsive and sub-complete. We transform I to an sf instance I'I′, and start by describing the nodes and the capacities in I'I′.For each single doctor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_i\in D$$\end{document}di∈D, we just
